T,\vartheta,S
and write
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}
and
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
for the respective projections on
A^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}
and
A^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
(to be defined in this proof). Then we put
\Pi=\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\otimes\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
. We are going to use the decomposition (
7.2
) of the entropies into a loop part and a shred part.

Note that, on any measurable space
\Omega
,

H(P\circ f^{-1}\mid Q\circ f^{-1})\leq H(P\mid Q),\qquad P,Q\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(\Omega),f\colon\Omega\to\Omega\mbox{ mb.},

(7.24)

since the left-hand side is equal to the relative entropy of
P
with respect to
Q
on the
\sigma
-field generated by
f
, and relative entropies are increasing in the
\sigma
-field
[
G11
, Prop. 15.5(c)]
.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle H_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}

\displaystyle\big(\xi\circ\Pi^{-1}\mid{\tt Q}_{W}\otimes[\partial(\xi\circ\Pi^{-1})\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]\big)

(7.25)

\displaystyle=H_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}\big(\xi\circ\Pi^{-1}\mid[{\tt Q}_{W}\otimes[\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]]\circ\Pi^{-1}\big)+\int{\rm d}\xi\circ\Pi^{-1}\,\log\frac{{\rm d}[{\tt Q}_{W}\otimes[\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]]\circ\Pi^{-1}}{{\rm d}{\tt Q}_{W}\otimes[\partial(\xi\circ\Pi^{-1})\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]}

\displaystyle\leq H_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}\big(\xi\mid{\tt Q}_{W}\otimes[\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]\big)+\int_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}}{\rm d}\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\,\log\frac{{\rm d}[{\tt Q}_{W}\circ(\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}})^{-1}]}{{\rm d}{\tt Q}_{W}}\circ\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}{\rm d}\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\,\log\frac{{\rm d}[\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]\circ(\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}})^{-1}}{{\rm d}[\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]}\circ\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}-\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}{\rm d}\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\,\log\frac{{\rm d}[\partial(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\circ(\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}})^{-1})\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]}{{\rm d}\partial\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\otimes{\tt K}_{W}}\circ\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}.

Note that, in the last term on the right-hand side of (
7.25
), we can drop the
{\tt K}_{W}
-terms and see that this is a relative entropy, which is nonnegative; hence we can drop it with “
\leq
”.

Now we handle the second term on the right-hand side of (
7.25
). We use the projection
\Pi=\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},M,L,K}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\to A^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},M,L,K}
defined, for all
\omega=\sum_{x\in\zeta}\delta_{(x,f_{x})}
, by

\displaystyle\Pi(\omega)

\displaystyle=\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},M,L,K}(\omega)=\sum_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{l\in[L]}\Big[{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{|\zeta^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}_{z}|\leq K\}\sum_{x\in\zeta_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}\delta_{(x,f_{x})}

\displaystyle+{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{|\zeta^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}_{z}|>K\}\frac{1}{\binom{|\zeta^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}_{z}|}{K}}\sum_{B\subset\zeta^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}_{z}\colon|B|=K}\sum_{x\in B}\delta_{(x,f_{x})}\Big],

where
\zeta^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}_{z}=\{x\in\zeta\cap(z+W_{\mathfrak{r}})\colon\ell(f_{x})=l,f_{x}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\circlearrowleft}})}}}\}
, where
{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\circlearrowleft}})}}}
denotes the set of all loops all of whose legs have a spread
\leq M
. In other words,
\Pi
first removes all loops that are longer than
L
or have a leg with spread
>M
and then removes in each subbox
z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}
and for any
l\in[L]
, if necessary, a uniformly distributed choice of loops such that no more than
K
loops of length
=l
survive. This algorithm can be conceived as being carried out independently in each of these subboxes.

Recall that
{\mathcal{L}}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}
denotes the set of loop configurations in
{\mathcal{L}}_{W}
(i.e., with all particles in
W
) that have only legs in
{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}
, i.e., with all the spreads
\leq M
. With an additional super-index
l
we restrict to configurations with all loop lengths
=l
, and the super-index
\leq L
means the union over
l\in[L]=\{1,\dots,L\}
. With the index
W;\mathfrak{r},W^{\prime}
for
W^{\prime}\subset W
we mean those configurations with particles in
W
that have initial site in
W^{\prime}
. All this notation also applies to measures
{\tt Q}
.

For
\omega
in the image of
\Pi
, the pre-image
\Pi^{-1}(\{\omega\})
is equal to the set of configurations of the form
\sum_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(\omega_{z}+\omega_{z}^{\prime}+\sum_{l\in[L]}\omega_{z,l}^{\prime\prime})
, where
\omega_{z}=\sum_{x\in\zeta\cap(z+W_{\mathfrak{r}})}\delta_{(x,f_{x})}
(the projection of
\omega
to that subbox), and
\omega^{\prime}_{z}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}\setminus{\mathcal{L}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\leq L}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
and
\omega_{z,l}^{\prime\prime}=0
(empty configuration) if
|\zeta_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}|<K
and
\omega_{z,l}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
otherwise. Note that all the configurations
\omega_{z}^{\prime}
and
\omega_{z,l}^{\prime\prime}
are independent over
z
and
l
under
{\tt Q}_{W}
. For fixed
z
and
l
, we split
{\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
into the PPP
{\tt Q}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\infty,l}})}}}\setminus{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}
. The first one has an intensity measure with total mass

q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}=\frac{1}{l}\int_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}{\rm d}x\,\mu_{x,x}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l,W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}}),

(recall (
1.6
)), where
\mu_{x,x}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l,W,{\rm par}}})}}}
is the restriction of
\mu_{x,x}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l,W,{\rm par}}})}}}
to loops configurations with loops in the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\circlearrowleft}})}}}=\{f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}}\colon\forall i\colon f_{x,i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{M}\}
. These two PPPs are independent. Therefore, the density
f
of
{\tt Q}_{W}\circ\Pi^{-1}
with respect to
{\tt Q}_{W}
is the product

f(\omega)=\prod_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\Big[{\operatorname{e}}^{\sum_{l=L+1}^{\infty}q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}}\prod_{l\in[L]}f_{z,l}(\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}})\Big]={\operatorname{e}}^{\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{>L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}})}\prod_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\prod_{l\in[L]}f_{z,l}(\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}),

(7.26)

with a density
f_{z,l}
of
{\tt Q}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}\circ\Pi_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{-1}
with respect to
{\tt Q}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}
, where
{\tt Q}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
denotes the PPP on the set
{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
of loop configurations with starting sites in
z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}
and all the particles contained in
W
. The
q
-term expresses that, for
l>L
, the pre-image of the empty configuration is equal to
{\mathcal{L}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
and the empty configuration has probability
{\operatorname{e}}^{-q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}}
under
{\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
. Recall that
\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}
denotes the intensity measure of the PPP on
W
, and we wrote
{\mathcal{C}}_{>L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}}
for the set of loops with lengths
>L
.

Hence, the only difference between the distribution of a given
\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}\setminus{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}
under
{\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\Pi^{-1}
and under
{\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}
is the change of the Poisson parameter from
q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}
to
q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}=q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\infty,l}})}}}
. Therefore, for
\omega_{z}
with
|\zeta_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}|<K
, we have

f_{z,l}(\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}})=\frac{{\rm d}{\tt Q}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}{{\rm d}{\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}}(\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}})={\operatorname{e}}^{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}-q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}\Big(\frac{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}}\Big)^{|\zeta_{z,l}|},

where
\zeta_{z,l}
is the set of initial sites of the loops in
z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}
with length
l
.

For
\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}
with
|\zeta_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}|=K
, in the pre-image there may be additional loops in
{\mathcal{L}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}
with uniformly and independently distributed locations, such that an additional Poisson density comes in, but also a combinatorial term that assigns the
m
points to the additional loops and the other
K
to the existing loop:

\displaystyle f_{z,l}(\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}{\tt Q}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}{{\rm d}{\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}}(\omega_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}})\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{{\rm Poi}_{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}(K+m)}{{\rm Poi}_{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}(K){\rm Poi}_{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}(m)}{\tt Q}_{W;\mathfrak{r},z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\{\omega_{z}^{\prime}\colon|\zeta_{z,l}|=m\})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{e}}^{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}-q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}\Big(\frac{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}}\Big)^{|\zeta_{z,l}|}{\operatorname{e}}^{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}={\operatorname{e}}^{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}}\Big(\frac{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}}\Big)^{|\zeta_{z,l}|}.

Recall that we picked
\mathfrak{r}
such that
W
is the disjoint (up to boundaries) union of the subboxes
z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}
with
z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Therefore
\sum_{z}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}=\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}})
and
\sum_{z}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}=\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\circlearrowleft}})}}})
, the total mass of the reference measure of the set of all loop configurations with all spreads of the legs
\leq M
. Therefore, we can summarize

\displaystyle f(\omega)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{e}}^{\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{>L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}})}{\operatorname{e}}^{\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{\leq L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}})-\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{\leq L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\circlearrowleft}})}}})}\prod_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\prod_{l\in[L]}\Big[\Big(\frac{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}}{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l}})}}}}\Big)^{|\zeta_{z,l}|}{\operatorname{e}}^{q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{|\zeta_{z,l}|=K\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq{\operatorname{e}}^{\varepsilon_{M,W,L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}|W|}\exp\Big\{\sum_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{l\in[L]}q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{|\zeta_{z,l}|=K\}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq{\operatorname{e}}^{\varepsilon_{M,W,L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}|W|}\exp\Big\{\frac{1}{K}\sum_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{l\in[L]}q_{z}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,l}})}}}|\zeta_{z,l}|\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq{\operatorname{e}}^{\varepsilon_{M,W,L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}|W|}{\operatorname{e}}^{\frac{|\zeta|}{K}},

where we put
\varepsilon_{M,W,L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}=\frac{1}{|W|}[\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,{\rm par}}})}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{>L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}})+\nu^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm par}})}}}(W\times[{\mathcal{C}}_{\leq L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\circlearrowleft}})}}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{\leq L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\circlearrowleft}})}}}])]
.

Hence, the second term on the right-hand side of (
7.25
) can be estimated as follows.

\displaystyle\int

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\,\log\Big(\frac{{\rm d}({\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\circ(\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}})^{-1})}{{\rm d}{\tt Q}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}}\circ\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\Big)=\int{\rm d}\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\,\log f(\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(\omega))\leq|W|\varepsilon_{M,W,L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}+\frac{1}{K}\int{\rm d}\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\,|\zeta|

\displaystyle\leq|W|\varepsilon_{M,W,L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}+\frac{1}{K}\langle\xi,\mathfrak{N}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}_{W}\rangle.

We leave it to the reader to prove that
\lim_{L\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{W\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varepsilon_{M,W,L}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}=0
.

Now we turn to estimating the third term on the right-hand side of (
7.25
). We define
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{W}\to A_{W;\mathfrak{r},M,T}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
by describing in words the removal procedure as follows. From a given shred configuration
\varpi=\sum_{g\in\Gamma}\delta_{g}
, first all shreds are removed that do not lie in

{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\big\{f\in{\mathcal{C}}_{k}\colon f_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}},|f(i\beta)-f((i+S)\beta)|\leq\vartheta\sqrt{S}\ \forall i\big\}.

Then, with a fixed enumeration
(z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{N})
of
W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we successively for
i=1,\dots,N
remove shreds from the configuration such that the particle number in
z_{i}+W_{\mathfrak{r}}
is not larger than
T
. We write
{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
for the set of all configurations of
W
-shreds that do not lie in
{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
, i.e., the set of
\sum_{g\in\Gamma}\delta_{g}
with
g\in{\mathcal{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
for all
g\in\Gamma
.

For simplicity, we write
\Pi
instead of
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
. For a given
\varpi
in the image of
\Pi
, the pre-image
\Pi^{-1}(\{\varpi\})
is contained in the set of all
W
-shred configurations
\varpi+\varpi^{\prime}+\varpi^{\prime\prime}
with
\varpi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
and
\varpi^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
being either the empty configuration or satisfying
\widetilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\varpi^{\prime\prime}+\varpi)>T
for some
z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Denote
{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}_{z,\varpi}=\{\varpi^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}\colon\widetilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\varpi^{\prime\prime})>T-\widetilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\varpi)\}
, then

\Pi^{-1}(\{\varpi\})\subset\{\varpi\}\cup\Big(\{\varpi\}+({\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}\setminus\{0\})+\bigcup_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}_{z,\varpi}\Big).

Then, writing
\partial\xi
instead of
\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi)
and
\mu
for
\partial\varpi
and
{\tt K}
instead of
{\tt K}_{W}
, we have, using the product structure of
{\tt K}
,

\displaystyle(\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K})\circ\Pi^{-1}({\rm d}\varpi)

\displaystyle\leq\partial\xi({\rm d}\mu){\tt K}(\mu,{\rm d}\varpi)\Big[1+

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}}\frac{\xi({\rm d}\mu+{\rm d}\mu^{\prime})}{\partial\xi({\rm d}\mu)}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{|\mu^{\prime}|\geq 1\}\int_{({\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}\setminus\{0\})+\bigcup_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}_{z,\varpi}}{\tt K}(\mu^{\prime},{\rm d}\varpi^{\prime})\Big].

Hence, the third term on the right-hand side of (
7.25
) can be estimated as follows (using
\log(1+x)\leq x
):

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}{\rm d}\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}

\displaystyle\log\frac{{\rm d}[\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]\circ(\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}})^{-1}}{{\rm d}[\partial\xi\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]}\circ\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}

(7.27)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}\xi({\rm d}\varpi)\int_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}}\frac{\partial\xi({\rm d}\mu+{\rm d}\mu^{\prime})}{\partial\xi({\rm d}\mu)}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{|\mu^{\prime}|\geq 1\}{\tt K}\Big(\mu^{\prime},({\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}\setminus\{0\})+\bigcup_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}_{z,\Pi(\varpi)}\Big).

Since
{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
and
\bigcup_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}_{z,\Pi(\varpi)}
are disjoint sets of
W
-shreds, its elements can be written as the sum over configurations of these two sets. Depending on these two,
\mu^{\prime}
can be split into the sum of
\widetilde{\mu}=\sum_{i\in\widetilde{I}}\delta_{(x_{i},l_{i},y_{i})}
and
\mu^{\prime}\setminus\widetilde{\mu}=\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}\delta_{(x_{i},l_{i},y_{i})}
, and the last term in the last display decomposes into the according product of
{\tt K}(\widetilde{\mu},{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}\setminus\{0\})
and
{\tt K}(\mu^{\prime}\setminus\widetilde{\mu},\bigcup_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}_{z,\Pi(\varpi)})
. We estimate the second term as follows.

\displaystyle{\tt K}\Big(\mu^{\prime}\setminus\widetilde{\mu},\bigcup_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}_{{z,\Pi(\varpi)}}(\Pi(\varpi))\Big)

\displaystyle\leq{\tt K}\Big(\mu^{\prime}\setminus\widetilde{\mu},\bigcup_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\{\varpi^{\prime}\colon\widetilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\varpi^{\prime})>T-\widetilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\Pi(\varpi))\}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{z\in W\cap 2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}{\tt K}(\mu^{\prime}\setminus\widetilde{\mu},{\rm d}\varpi^{\prime})\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\varpi^{\prime}+\Pi(\varpi))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\Big[\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\varpi)+\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}\setminus\widetilde{I}}l_{i}\Big],

where in the last step we used that
\Pi(\varpi)\leq\varpi
and that
W
is the (disjoint, up to boundaries) union of the boxes
z+W_{\mathfrak{r}}
,
z\in W+2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

For estimating the other, we use that the probability of the set of single
W
-shreds of length
k
, namely
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
under
{\tt K}(\delta_{(x,l,y)},\cdot)
for any
(x,l,y)
has a probability
\geq(1-\varepsilon_{M})^{k}(1-\varepsilon_{\vartheta,S})^{k/S}
for some
\varepsilon_{M}
and
\varepsilon_{\vartheta,S}
that vanish if
M\to\infty
respectively
\vartheta\to\infty
(both do not depend on
R
). The reason is that all spreads of the legs and all the differences
g(iS\beta)-g((i-1)S\beta)
are independent. Since these
W
-shreds are independent under
{\tt K}(\widetilde{\mu},\cdot)
, we can estimate
{\tt K}(\widetilde{\mu},{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{\neg{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}\setminus\{0\})\leq 1-\prod_{i\in\widetilde{I}}(1-\varepsilon_{M})^{l_{i}}(1-\varepsilon_{\vartheta,S})^{l_{i}/S}\leq C(\varepsilon_{M}+\varepsilon_{\vartheta,S})\sum_{i\in\widetilde{I}}l_{i}
.

We estimate now the product against the maximum of these two upper bounds (also depending on whether
\widetilde{\mu}=0
or
\mu^{\prime}\setminus\widetilde{\mu}=0
) and the other one against one, and for simplicity the entire term against the sum of the two upper bounds. Substituting this above, we see that the right-hand side of (
7.27
) is not larger than

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}\xi({\rm d}\varpi)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}}\frac{\partial\xi({\rm d}\mu+{\rm d}\mu^{\prime})}{\partial\xi({\rm d}\mu)}\,\Big[C(\varepsilon_{M}+\varepsilon_{\vartheta,S})\sum_{i\in\widetilde{I}}l_{i}+\frac{1}{T}\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\varpi)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}\ l_{i}\Big]\leq\varepsilon_{M,\vartheta,S,T}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\langle\xi,\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\rangle,

with an obvious choice of
\varepsilon_{M,\vartheta,S,T}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
such that
\lim_{M,\vartheta,T\to\infty}\varepsilon_{M,\vartheta,S,T}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}=0
for any
S
.
∎

7.5.
Ergodic approximation

In the main result of this section, Lemma
7.6
, we prove a technical, but crucial approximation that will be used in several important other proofs. The main message is that an arbitrary shift-invariant loop-interlacement distribution can be replaced by an ergodic measure that has only loops and interlacements with additional nice properties, without changing the crucial properties (particle densities in loops and in interlacements, interaction energy, entropy) much. More precisely, in the crucial variational formula
\chi(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
in (
2.24
), we show that the infimum can be restricted, with only a small error, to the set of ergodic random configurations such that their projection on
W=[-R,R]^{d}
is concentrated on the event
A_{W;\mathfrak{r},M,L,K,T}
defined in (
4.34
), (
4.35
) and (
4.36
) and on
{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
(defined in (
5.21
)) if
M,L,K,T,\vartheta
are picked large enough, uniformly in
S\in\mathbb{N}
. Here we will use Lemma
7.5
to handle the entropy part of the variational formula.

Lemma
7.6
is crucial for applying several preliminary results that rely on such special properties of the random configurations, since these imply for example that the error term in the large-deviations lower bound in Lemma
5.5
can be controlled and that the interaction energy per unit volume is bounded. More explicitly, Lemma
7.6
will be used in the proof of the lower bound of Theorem
2.5
in Section
9.2
(it is what we need in order to go on from (
6.35
))
and in the proof of Theorem
2.3
in Section
8
and was already used in the proof of Lemma
2.12
in Section
7.1
.

We follow a similar ansatz as in the proof of Lemma
2.13
. Indeed, for a given loop/shred configuration in a given large box, we do not change anything in the configuration in the box, but we rewire all the ends of the shreds (the loop shreds and the interlacement shreds) in such a way that they are now connected with certain regularly placed sites at the boundary of a slightly larger box. Doing this independently in all these slightly larger subboxes (which decompose
\mathbb{R}^{d}
), they connect up with the new shreds in the neighbouring subbox. This leads to a global configuration whose crucial three quantities (entropy, interaction energy and particle densities) are hardly changed and which has much better independence properties, in particular, is ergodic.

Lemma 7.6

(Ergodic approximation)
.

Fix
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in(0,\infty)
and fix
P\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
with finite
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)
and finite
\langle F_{U},P\rangle
and
\langle P,{\mathfrak{N}}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\rangle=\rho_{1},\langle P,{\mathfrak{N}}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\rangle=\rho_{2}
. Furthermore, fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Then, for any sufficiently large
\vartheta,M,L,K,T,R
(in this order), there is an ergodic
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
such that
\Pi_{W_{R+M}}(P^{\prime})(A_{{W_{R+M}};\mathfrak{r},M,L,K,T})=1=\Pi_{W_{R+M}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P^{\prime})(S_{W_{R+M}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}),\langle P^{\prime},\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\rangle\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{1}),\langle P^{\prime},\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\rangle\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{2})
and
\frac{1}{|{W_{R+M}}|}\langle F_{{W_{R+M}},{W_{R+M}}},\Pi_{W_{R+M}}(P^{\prime})\rangle\leq\varepsilon+\langle F_{U},P\rangle
and
J_{W_{R+M}}(\Pi_{W_{R+M}}(P^{\prime}))\leq\varepsilon+J_{W_{R}}(\Pi_{W_{R}}(P))
.

Proof.

Our proof is in the spirit of the one of Lemma
2.13
in Section
7.3
. Again we abbreviate

W=W_{R}=[-R,R]^{d}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\widetilde{W}=W_{R+M}=[-R-M,R+M]^{d}\qquad\mbox{and put}\qquad W^{\prime}=\widetilde{W}\setminus W.

As usual,
\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}
is the well-known projection on
\widetilde{W}
. Denote by
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\widetilde{W}}=\Pi_{\widetilde{W};\mathfrak{r},M,L,K,T}
the projection onto
A_{\widetilde{W};\mathfrak{r},M,L,K,T}
introduced in Lemma
7.5
under the name
\Pi
(with
\widetilde{W}
instead of
W
). We pick
M,K,L,\vartheta,T
so large that the particle densities of the
\widetilde{\Pi}
-projection of
P
are close enough to the ones of
P
and the error terms in Lemma
7.5
are small enough, more precisely,

|\langle\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{U},\Pi_{U}(P)\rangle-\rho_{1}|\leq\varepsilon
and
|\langle\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{U},\Pi_{U}(P)\rangle-\rho_{2}|\leq\varepsilon
and that
\lim_{R^{\prime}\to\infty}\varepsilon^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{M,[-R^{\prime},R^{\prime}]^{d},L}+(\frac{1}{K}+\varepsilon_{M,\vartheta,S,T}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}})(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}+1)\leq\varepsilon
.

Then, for any sufficiently large
R
we will construct an ergodic
\widetilde{P}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\widetilde{W}|}\langle F_{\widetilde{W},\widetilde{W}}\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{\widetilde{W}},\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P})\rangle-\frac{1}{|W|}\langle F_{W,W}\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W},\Pi_{W}(P)\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon,

(7.28)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\widetilde{W}|}\langle[\mathfrak{N}_{\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}+\mathfrak{N}_{\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}]\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{\widetilde{W}},\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P})\rangle-\frac{1}{|W|}\langle[\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}+\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}]\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W},\Pi_{W}(P)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon,

(7.29)

\displaystyle J_{\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P}))-J_{W}(\Pi_{W}(P)\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W}^{-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon.

(7.30)

This will end the proof of this lemma with
P^{\prime}=\widetilde{P}\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{\widetilde{W}}^{-1}
, since
\frac{1}{|W|}\langle F_{W,W}\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W},\Pi_{W}(P)\rangle\leq\langle F_{U},P\rangle
and, if
R
is large enough,
J_{\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P})\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{\widetilde{W}}^{-1})\leq\varepsilon+J_{\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P}))
(see Lemma
7.5
and our choice of
M,K,L;T
above), and
J_{W}(\Pi_{W}(P)\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W}^{-1})\leq J_{W}(\Pi_{W}(P))+\varepsilon
again by Lemma
7.5
.

Let us now construct
\widetilde{P}
. This will be partially similar to the proof of Lemma
2.13
. Again, we extend the
W
-projection of
P
to a random configuration in
\widetilde{W}
, but here we do it in such a way that all
W
-shreds are extended to the boundary of
\widetilde{W}
in such a way that their composition in all the boxes
z+\widetilde{W}
with
z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
concatenates to interlacements; this time we are not creating new loops. We do this for all shreds, also the loop shreds, since we will later show that the expected number of particles in these loops is negligible in the limit
W\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
; hence it will not change much in the expected particle numbers if we turn all loops that have particles outside
W
into interlacements.

Decompose
\mathbb{R}^{d}
regularly into boxes
\widetilde{W}_{z}=z+\widetilde{W}
with
z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and assume that
R
is much larger than
M
. Put
\xi_{R}=\Pi_{W}(P)\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W}^{-1}

and
\psi_{R}=\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi_{R})
. We will construct a loop/shred configuration
\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{\widetilde{W}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{\widetilde{W}})
that extends
\xi_{R}
by appending independent Brownian
W^{\prime}
-shreds in such a way that no new loop (or hardly any) is created in
\widetilde{W}
and all
W
-shreds are connected with
\partial\widetilde{W}
in a certain regular way. We will do this independently over
z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, such that all these added shreds in neighbouring subboxes automatically connect up such that they are concatenated to interlacements in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

For this, pick some deterministic index set
\Phi
with
|\Phi|\asymp|W|/R\asymp R^{d-1}
and a collection of points
(X_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
in
W_{R+M}^{\circ}\setminus W_{R+M-1}
that are more than one away from the corners of
W_{R+M}
, i.e., from
(R+M)\{-1,1\}^{d}
. Then each
X_{a}
is closest to precisely one of the
2d
neighbouring subboxes
z+W_{R+M}
with
z\in 2(R+M)\{-1,1\}^{d}
(i.e., there is precisely one
z\in 2(R+M)\{-1,1\}^{d}
such that
X_{a}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(\widetilde{W}\cap(z+\widetilde{W}))
, where
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
denotes the 1-neighbourhood). Assume that, for any
z\in 2(R+M)\{-1,1\}^{d}
, the number of
a
such that
X_{a}
is closest to
z+W
is the same. Put
Y_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}}=X_{a}
for these
z
.
Furthermore, put
X_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}}=z+X_{a}
for
z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and define
Y_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z^{\prime}}})}}}
as above for any neighbour
z^{\prime}
of
z
and
X_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}}
. We conceive, for any
z\in 2(R+M)Z^{d}
, the pair
(X_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}},Y_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}})\in(z+\widetilde{W},(z+\widetilde{W})^{\rm c})
as the entry-exit sites of a
(z+\widetilde{W})
-shred. They lie perfectly opposite to each other close to the boundary of
z+\widetilde{W}
. We put
X_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}}=X_{a}
and
Y_{a}=Y_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}}
.

Now let
\mu=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{(x_{i},l_{i},y_{i})}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W}
be given such that
y_{i}\in\widetilde{W}
for all
i\in I
. We are considering
\mu
under
\partial\Pi_{W}^{{}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}}(\xi_{R})
, i.e., we can see
\mu
as the configuration of all the starting/terminal sites and lengths of all the
W
-shreds in the configuration that is sampled under
\xi_{R}
. Note that
\xi_{R}
has, by definition as a random configuration in the event
A_{W;\mathfrak{r},M,L,K;T}
, with probability one, only a bounded number of particles in any subbox of radius
\mathfrak{r}
around sites in
2\mathfrak{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and hence not more than
\asymp R^{d-1}
particles close to
\partial W
. Therefore, we may assume that
|I|\leq O(R^{d-1})
.

We pick an injective map
I\ni i\mapsto a_{i}\in\Phi
and connect
X_{a_{i}}
with
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
with
Y_{a_{i}}
by means of an independent Brownian
W^{\prime}
-shred, for any
i\in I
. For all other
a
’s, i.e., for
a\in\Phi\setminus I_{\Phi}
with
I_{\Phi}=\{a_{i}\colon i\in I\}
, we connect
X_{a}
with
Y_{a}
with an additional independent Brownian
W^{\prime}
-shred. The distribution of the resulting configuration is called
\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}
, i.e., the extension of
\xi_{R}
to a loop/shred configuration in
\widetilde{W}
without loops in
W^{\prime}
.

Having constructed
\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}
in this way, we take independent measures
\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,z}})}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{\widetilde{W}_{z}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{\widetilde{W}_{z}})
with
z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that their shift
\theta_{z}(\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,z}})}}})
has the same distribution as
\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}
. Then we put

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{R,M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,z}})}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}),

(7.31)

\displaystyle P_{R,M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\widetilde{W}|}\int_{\widetilde{W}}{\rm d}x\,\theta_{x}(\widetilde{P}_{R,M})\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{\widetilde{W}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{\widetilde{W}}),

(7.32)

the latter which is obviously stationary. The proof of
[
G11
, Theorem 14.12]
applies and shows that
P_{R,M}
is ergodic. We will show that
\widetilde{P}=P_{R,M}
satisfies (
7.28
)–(
7.30
) for
R
large enough, which ends the proof of the lemma.

Note that
P_{R,M}
has its interlacements only from our construction from the
|\Phi|
-many connections of
X_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}}
with
Y_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}}
(partially via
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
), which perfectly connect each other in the sites
Y_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z}})}}}=X_{a}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({z^{\prime}}})}}}
, if
z+\widetilde{W}
and
z^{\prime}+\widetilde{W}
are neighbouring subboxes. All the loops of
P_{R,M}
are the
(z+W)
-loops for some
z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

The proofs of (
7.28
) and (
7.29
) are more or less identical to the proofs of (
7.11
) and (
7.12
) in the proof of Lemma
2.13
; we leave the details to the reader; let us only mention that here also the following Lemma
7.7
is used, which says that our decision to turn all
W
-loops that have particles also in
W^{\prime}
into interlacements does not noticeably change the particle densities:
For shift-invariant loop-distributions, the expected number of particles in loops that have particles in
W
, but not all their particles, is negligible:

Lemma 7.7
.

For any
P\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}})
with
\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{|W_{R}|}\langle\Pi_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P),\mathfrak{N}_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle=0.

Proof.

Note that
\Pi_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)=\Pi_{W_{R}}(P)-\Pi_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(P)
. By shift-invariance, for
R\in\mathbb{N}
,

\frac{1}{|W_{R}|}\langle\Pi_{W_{R}}(P),\mathfrak{N}_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle=\frac{1}{|W_{R}|}\sum_{z\in W_{R}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\langle\Pi_{W_{R}}(P),\mathfrak{N}_{z+U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle=\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle.

Furthermore, the normalized integral with respect to the second is asymptotically not smaller than
\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle
. Indeed, denoting by
\mathfrak{N}_{\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,\leq M}})}}}
the number of particles in all the loops
f
that start in
\widetilde{W}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and have spread
\max_{t\in[0,\beta\ell(f)]}|f(t)-f(0)|\leq M
, we see that

\frac{1}{|W_{R}|}\langle\Pi_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(P),\mathfrak{N}_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle\geq\frac{1}{|W_{R}|}\sum_{z\in W_{R-M}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\langle\Pi_{W_{R}}(P),\mathfrak{N}_{z+U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,\leq M}})}}}\rangle=\frac{|W_{R-M}|}{|W_{R}|}\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,\leq M}})}}}\rangle\to\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,\leq M}})}}}\rangle

as
R\to\infty
, and the last term converges to
\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle
as
M\to\infty
, according to the monotonous convergence theorem.
∎

Proof of (
7.30
):
Analogously to the argument around (
7.14
), we can argue also here that it suffices to show that
J_{\widetilde{W}}(\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}})\leq J_{W}(\xi_{R})+\varepsilon
. Indeed, the estimate in (
7.14
) is analogously valid here as well, with the difference that the second error term on the right-hand side of (
6.17
) is not trivial as in (
7.14
) (since
\psi=0
there), but needs an argument, since
\psi=\partial\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\widetilde{P}_{R,M})
is non-trivial: For large
R
we have

\displaystyle J_{\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(P_{R,M}))

\displaystyle\leq J_{3\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P}_{R,M}))+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}-\langle\psi,\mathfrak{p}_{3\widetilde{W},M}\rangle.

(7.33)

In order to show that the last term is negligible, we point out that all the
3\widetilde{W}
-shreds
f_{i}^{\prime}
of
\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P}_{R,M})
satisfy the condition
|f_{i}^{\prime}(jS\beta)-f_{i}^{\prime}((j-1)S\beta)/\leq\vartheta\sqrt{S}
either almost surely for those
j
such that
f_{i}^{\prime}([(j-1)S\beta,jS\beta])
lies in
z+W
for some
z\in 2(R+M)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(since they come from
\xi_{R}
or an independent copy of it, which is concentrated on
A_{W_{R};\Theta}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
) or in expectation, since these pieces are independent Brownian bridges by construction (see the argument in Remark
5.6
). Therefore, making
S
large enough, we may assume that
J_{\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(P_{R,M}))\leq J_{3\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P}_{R,M}))+\varepsilon
.

In order to see that
J_{3\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P}_{R,M}))=J_{\widetilde{W}}(\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{P}_{R,M}))=J_{\widetilde{W}}(\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}})
, observe that the configurations in the
3^{d}
subboxes
z+\widetilde{W}
with
z\in 2(R+M)\{-1,0,1\}^{d}
are independent copies from each other and that therefore the density of
\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}(P)(\omega,\mu,\cdot)
with respect to
{\tt K}_{3\widetilde{W}}(\mu,\cdot)
, conditional on the boundary-shred configuration
\mu
, decomposes into a product of the corresponding
(z+\widetilde{W})
-versions (since
\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}(P)
decomposes into i.i.d. parts by definition, and
{\tt K}_{3\widetilde{W}}
decomposes by the Markov property), and therefore the entropy of
\Pi_{3\widetilde{W}}(P)
decomposes into the sum over these entropies. Because of the prefactor
\frac{1}{|\widetilde{W}|}
in the definition of
J_{\widetilde{W}}
, we obtain that the
J_{3\widetilde{W}}
-value equals the
J_{\widetilde{W}}
-value. Hence, for proving (
7.30
), we only need to show that
J_{\widetilde{W}}(\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}})\leq J_{W}(\xi_{R})+\varepsilon
.

The distribution of
\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}
can be written, using the Markov property, as follows. Let
(\omega,\varpi)\in{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}
with
\varpi=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{f_{i}}
and
\mu=\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\varpi)=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{(x_{i},l_{i},y_{i})}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W}
. Recall that
|I|\leq|\Phi|
and that we did not add any
\widetilde{W}
-loops. We can write, using the Markov property and the independence of all the Brownian
W^{\prime}
-shreds,

\displaystyle\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}\big({\rm d}(\omega^{\prime},\varpi^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=\xi_{R}\big({\rm d}(\omega,\varpi)\big)\,{\operatorname{e}}^{-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}[q_{W^{\prime},k}+q_{W^{\prime},W,k}]}

(7.34)

\displaystyle\otimes\bigotimes_{i\in I}\Big[p_{X_{a_{i}},x_{i}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}m_{i})q_{X_{a_{i}},x_{i}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{i},W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}g_{i})\,p_{y_{i},Y_{a_{i}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}m_{i}^{\prime})q_{y_{i},Y_{a_{i}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{i}^{\prime},W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}g_{i}^{\prime})\Big]

\displaystyle\otimes\bigotimes_{a\in\Phi\setminus I_{\Phi}}\Big[p^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W^{\prime}}})}}}_{X_{a},Y_{a}}({\rm d}n_{a})q^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n_{a},W^{\prime}}})}}}_{X_{a},Y_{a}}({\rm d}g_{a}^{\prime})\Big],

where we remind on the definition of the
p
- and the
q
-terms in (
7.15
) and (
7.16
). We used the notation
\omega^{\prime}=\omega
(seen as an element of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\widetilde{W}}
with only
W
-loops) and
\varpi^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{g_{i}^{\prime}\diamond f_{i}\diamond g_{i}}+\sum_{a\in\Phi\setminus I_{\Phi}}\delta_{g_{a}^{\prime}}
and
\mu^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{(X_{a_{i}},L_{a_{i}},Y_{a_{i}})}+\sum_{a\in\Phi\setminus I_{\Phi}}\delta_{(X_{a},n_{a},Y_{a})}
with
L_{a_{i}}=m_{i}+\ell(f_{i})+m_{i}^{\prime}
.

Analogously, we write the distribution of the reference measure, using the above notation. Recall from the proof of Lemma
2.13
that the reference measure
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{\widetilde{W}}({\tt Q})
is the convolution of
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{W}({\tt Q})
,
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{W^{\prime}}({\tt Q})
and
\Pi_{W,W^{\prime}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\tt Q})
(recall that the latter measure is the restriction of
{\tt Q}
to the set of loops that have all particles in
W\cup W^{\prime}
and have at least one in
W
and one in
W^{\prime}
). Hence, the loop part is similar to (
7.19
) without the
n
-loop, and we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\tt Q})

\displaystyle\otimes[\partial\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}})\otimes{\tt K}_{\widetilde{W}}]\big({\rm d}(\omega^{\prime},\varpi^{\prime})\big)=\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\tt Q})({\rm d}\omega)\,{\operatorname{e}}^{-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}[q_{W^{\prime},k}+q_{W^{\prime},W,k}]}\,{\operatorname{e}}^{-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q_{W,W^{\prime},k}}

(7.35)

\displaystyle\otimes\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi_{R})({\rm d}\mu)\bigotimes_{i\in I}\Big[p_{X_{a_{i}},x_{i}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}m_{i})q_{X_{a_{i}},x_{i}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{i},W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}g_{i})p_{y_{i},Y_{a_{i}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}m_{i}^{\prime})q_{y_{i},Y_{a_{i}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{i}^{\prime},W^{\prime}}})}}}({\rm d}g_{i}^{\prime})\Big]

\displaystyle\otimes\bigotimes_{a\in\Phi\setminus I_{\Phi}}\Big[p^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W^{\prime}}})}}}_{X_{a},Y_{a}}({\rm d}n_{a})q^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n_{a},W^{\prime}}})}}}_{X_{a},Y_{a}}({\rm d}g_{a}^{\prime})\Big]\otimes{\tt K}_{W}(\mu,{\rm d}\varpi).

Summarizing,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}}{{\rm d}\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\tt Q})\otimes[\partial\Pi_{\widetilde{W}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}})\otimes{\tt K}_{\widetilde{W}}]}(\omega^{\prime},\varpi^{\prime})=\frac{{\rm d}\xi_{R}}{{\rm d}\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\tt Q})\otimes[\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi_{R})\otimes{\tt K}_{W}]}(\omega,\varpi){\operatorname{e}}^{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q_{W,W^{\prime},k}}.

Hence, we see that

J_{\widetilde{W}}(\xi_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}})=J_{W}(\xi_{R})+\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q_{W,W^{\prime},k},

and as in the proof of Lemma
2.13
, one sees that the last term is
\leq\varepsilon
for
R
large enough. This ends the proof of (
7.30
) and hence the proof of this lemma.
∎

8.
Proof of Theorem
2.3
: specific relative entropy density

In this section we establish a new kind of
specific relative entropy per volume
of ergodic loop/interlacement configurations, namely with respect to the product of
\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\tt Q})
and the product of the boundary-configuration and the kernel
{\tt K}_{W}
defined in (
2.5
). In other words, we prove Theorem
2.3
. In particular, we identify the limit of the entropy term
J_{W}(\xi)
defined in (
2.13
) (see also (
6.13
)) appearing in the variational formula in (
6.33
) and (
6.35
) in the limit
R\to\infty
, i.e., in the limit
W=W_{R}=[-R,R]^{d}\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Recall that, in
d\geq 3
,
{\tt K}_{W}
is a regular version of the distribution of
\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}({\tt R})
given
\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}({\tt R})
for the Brownian
\beta
-spacing interlacement PPP,
{\tt R}
, of Section
A
, see Lemma
A.4
, but we are not going to use this fact.

Remark 8.1

The usual starting point of such a proof is to prove the superadditivity of the map
W\mapsto H_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}(\Pi_{W}(P)\mid\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({{\tt Q}})\otimes[\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)\otimes{\tt K}_{W}])
on the set of all boxes
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but we did not succeed in doing this, as it concerns the interlacement part. Instead, we will be relying on Proposition
6.6
, which needs ergodicity, combined with Lemma
7.6
, which approximates arbitrary stationary
P
’s by ergodic ones that have several useful additional properties.

\Diamond

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

Let
P
be in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
with finite expected particle number in the unit box
U
.

(1) We first prove that the limit in (
2.14
) exists and satisfies (
2.16
).
Pick an
R\in(0,\infty)
and pick any sequence
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
tending to
\infty
. We would like to apply Proposition
6.6
(2) and (3) to get a lower bound for
\liminf_{n\to\infty}J_{W_{R_{n}}}(\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P))
that is close to
J_{W_{R}}(\Pi_{W_{R}}(P))
, but we can do this only for
W_{R}
-ergodic
P
’s that are concentrated on
{\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}}
(see (
5.21
)) for some
M,\vartheta,S
. Hence, we first need to replace
\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P)
by some measure like that, using Lemma
7.6
. Indeed, with parameters
\mathfrak{r},M,L,K,T,\vartheta,S
, we apply Lemma
7.6
(see also the proof of Lemma
7.5
) to see that, for any given small
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and any
n
there is an ergodic
P_{n}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}})
such that
\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P_{n})
is concentrated on
A^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}_{W_{R_{n}};M,\mathfrak{r},T}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{W_{R_{n}}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\vartheta,S}})}}}
and satisfies
|\langle P_{n}-P,\mathfrak{N}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}},\ell}})}}}_{W_{R_{n}}}\rangle|\leq\varepsilon|W_{R_{n}}|
such that

J_{W_{R_{n}}}(\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P))\geq J_{W_{R_{n}}}(\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P_{n}))-\varepsilon,

if the parameters
M,L,K,T,\vartheta
are picked large enough. Now we can apply Proposition
6.6
(2). Indeed, we write
R_{n}=m_{n}R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}
with
m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and
m_{n}\to\infty
and
R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}\downarrow R
as
n\to\infty
and get

\displaystyle J_{W_{R_{n}}}(\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P_{n}))

\displaystyle\geq J_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(\Pi_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(P_{n}))-\frac{C_{M}}{(m_{n}R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}})^{d/2}}-\Big[\frac{C_{M,\mathfrak{r},T}}{m_{n}R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}+\frac{C\vartheta^{2}}{S}\Big]\langle P_{n},\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}},\ell}})}}}\rangle.

We can pick
S=\vartheta^{3}
. Now we combine the last two displays and make
n\to\infty
and use that
|\langle P_{n}-P,\mathfrak{N}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}},\ell}})}}}_{W_{R_{n}}}\rangle|\leq\varepsilon|W_{R_{n}}|
and that both
P
and
P_{n}
are shift-invariant to get that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}J_{W_{R_{n}}}(\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P))

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}J_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(\Pi_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(P_{n}))-\frac{C}{\vartheta}\big(\varepsilon+\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}},\ell}})}}}\rangle\big)-\varepsilon.

(8.1)

We can make
M,K,L,T,\vartheta\to\infty
to obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}J_{W_{R_{n}}}(\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P))

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{M,K,L,T,\vartheta\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}J_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(\Pi_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(P_{n}))-\varepsilon

(8.2)

\displaystyle\geq J_{W_{R}}(\Pi_{W_{R}}(P))-\varepsilon,

where we used the following argument for the
J
-term. Indeed, we want to use Proposition
6.6
(3). Note that, from the construction of
P_{n}
for
W_{R_{n}}
in the proof of Lemma
7.6
, we see that
\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P_{n})=\Pi_{W_{R_{n}}}(P)\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W_{{R_{n}}}}^{-1}
, where
\widetilde{\Pi}_{W_{R_{n}}}
is the projection onto
A_{W_{R_{n}};M,L,K,T}
introduced in Lemma
7.5
. Therefore, it is easy to see that
P_{n}
weakly converges (in our usual sense that all the projections
\Pi_{W_{R^{\prime}}}(P_{n})
weakly converge,
R^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
) towards
P
if all the parameters
n,M,K,L,T,\vartheta
grow to
\infty
. Furthermore,
\Pi_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(P_{n})=\Pi_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(P)\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}^{-1}
. In order to see that, in this limit inferior the limit
P_{n}\Longrightarrow P
and the limit as
R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}\downarrow R
according to Proposition
6.6
(3) can be combined such that the last step of (
8.2
) appears, use first (
7.24
) for
f=\widetilde{\Pi}_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}
and then prove that the density
{\rm d}\nu_{n}\circ\widetilde{\Pi}_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}^{-1}/{\rm d}\nu_{n}
tends to one in the limit as
n,M,K,L,T,\vartheta\to\infty
, where
\nu_{n}={\tt Q}_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}\otimes[\partial\Pi_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}(P)\otimes{\tt K}_{W_{R^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({m_{n}}})}}}}}]
is the reference measure. We leave the proof of the latter to the reader.

Now (
8.2
) implies that the limit inferior is not smaller than the limit superior, i.e., the limit in (
2.14
) exists. Furthermore, from (
8.2
) we also see that (
2.16
) holds.

(2) Now we show that
P\mapsto{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)
is lower semicontinuous.
Recall that we are using on
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
the weak topology induced by all the
\Pi_{W}
, i.e.,
P_{n}
converges towards
P
if and only if
\Pi_{W}(P_{n})
weakly converges towards
\Pi_{W}(P)
for any
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We write
{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}
as
[{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{T}}_{W}]\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}
and write
(\Pi_{W}(P))_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{T}}_{W}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}(\omega,\mu,\cdot)
for the corresponding kernel (by disintegration). Also the map
P\mapsto(\Pi_{W}(P))_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{T}}_{W}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}(\omega,\mu,\cdot)
is continuous for any
\omega\in{\mathcal{L}}_{W}
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W}
. It follows from the characterisation of the entropy in (
2.7
) and the fact that limits of lower semi-continuous functions are lower semi-continuous that relative entropies are lower semi-continuous in the first argument. With the help of writing
\partial\xi=\partial\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}_{W}(P)
and
\xi_{\mu}({\rm d}(\omega,\varpi))=(\Pi_{W}(P))_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}(\mu,{\rm d}(\omega,\varpi))
and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{W}(P)=|W|J_{W}(\Pi_{W}(P))

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}}\partial\xi({\rm d}\mu)\,H_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}\big(\xi_{\mu}\mid\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\tt Q})\otimes{\tt K}_{W}(\mu,\cdot)\big)

(8.3)

and Fatou’s lemma, the assertion follows.

(3) Next, we show the convexity of the map
P\mapsto J_{W}(\Pi_{W}(P))
.
This
follows from the convexity of the map
(\mu,\nu)\mapsto H(\mu\mid\nu)
for any relative entropy
H
, see
[
G11
, Proposition 15.5(d)]
. Since limit of convex functions are convex, the convexity of
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\cdot)
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
follows. The proof of concavity given in the proof of
[
G11
, Proposition 15.14]
applies also here, using (
8.3
).
Indeed, if one replaces
P
by
sP_{1}+(1-s)P_{2}
for
s\in(0,1)
and
P_{1},P_{2}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
, then
{\mathcal{H}}_{W}(sP_{1}+(1-s)P_{2})
can be lower estimated (using the monotonicity of the logarithm) against
s{\mathcal{H}}_{W}(P_{1})+(1-s){\mathcal{H}}_{W}(P_{2})+s\log s+(1-s)\log(1-s)
, and dividing by
|W|
and letting
|W|\to\infty
implies the concavity of
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\cdot)
on the set of all ergodic
P\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
.

(4) Now we prove the compactness of the restricted level set of
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\cdot)
, given in (
2.17
).
Assume that
c\in\mathbb{R}
and a sequence of compact sets
K_{R}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{W_{R}}
with
R\in\mathbb{N}
are given (we take the sequence
(R_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
as
\mathbb{N}
without loss of generality).
Because of lower semicontinuity of
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\cdot)
and of
P\mapsto\langle P,\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}}\rangle
, the set in (
2.17
) is closed, hence we only need to prove its tightness. Pick a sequence
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
in that set. Using (
2.16
), we see that, for any
R\in\mathbb{N}
, the projections
\Pi_{W_{R}}(P_{n})
lie for any
n
in the restricted level set

\big\{\xi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{W_{R}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W_{R}})\colon\partial\Pi_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi)\in K_{R}(L),J_{W_{R}}(\xi)\leq c^{\prime}\big\},

(8.4)

for some
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. According to Lemma
6.7
(1), this set is compact. Therefore,
(\Pi_{W_{R}}(P_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight. This finishes the proof, because of the following argument: We can assume that
(\Pi_{W_{R}}(P_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges towards some
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W_{R}}})}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{W_{R}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W_{R}})
as
n\to\infty
. Using a standard diagonal argument, we can assume that the
n
-subsequence works for all the
R\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
(\Pi_{W_{R}}(P_{n}))_{R\in\mathbb{N}}
is consistent for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, also
(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W_{R}}})}}})_{R\in\mathbb{N}}
is consistent. Now Proposition
4.1
yields the existence of a
P\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
such that
\Pi_{W_{R}}(P)=\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W_{R}}})}}}
for any
R\in\mathbb{N}
. It is easy to see that
P_{n}
converges (along the chosen subsequence) towards
P
.
∎

Let us close this section with making some comments and remarks. First, let us state the following useful general inequality (which was already used in the proof of Theorem
2.3
).

For a probability measure
\nu
on the product of two Polish spaces
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
with projections
\Pi_{i}\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{X}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
, we write
\nu_{1\to 2}
for the Markov kernel from
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
into
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{X}}_{2})
that is defined by the disintegration formula

\nu\big({\rm d}(x_{1},x_{2})\big)=\Pi_{1}(\nu)({\rm d}x_{1})\nu_{1\to 2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{1}}})}}}({\rm d}x_{2}).

(8.5)

In other words,
\nu_{1\to 2}
is a regular version of the conditional distribution of
X_{2}
given
X_{1}
if
(X_{1},X_{2})
is a random vector with distribution
\nu
. Let us also recall the standard measure-theoretic notation
\mu\otimes K({\rm d}(x_{1},x_{2}))=\mu({\rm d}x_{1})\,K(x_{1},{\rm d}x_{2})
and
\mu K({\rm d}x_{2})=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}\mu({\rm d}x_{1})\,K(x_{1},{\rm d}x_{2})
for
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{X}}_{1})
and a kernel
K
from
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
into
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{X}}_{2})
. By
H_{1},H_{2}
and
H
we denote the relative entropies on the spaces
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}),{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{X}}_{2})
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{X}}_{2})
, respectively.

Lemma 8.2

(Entropy on products)
.

For any two Polish spaces
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
with projections
\Pi_{i}\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{X}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
,

H(\nu\mid\rho)\geq H_{1}(\Pi_{1}(\nu)\mid\Pi_{1}(\rho))+H_{2}(\Pi_{1}(\nu)\nu_{1\to 2}\mid\Pi_{1}(\nu)\rho_{1\to 2}),\qquad\nu,\rho\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{X}}_{2}).

(8.6)

Proof.

We may assume that
\nu
has a density with respect to
\rho
. Then

\displaystyle H(\nu\mid\rho)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}\Pi_{1}(\nu)({\rm d}x_{1})\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}}\nu_{1\to 2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{1}}})}}}({\rm d}x_{2})\,\log\Big(\frac{{\rm d}(\Pi_{1}(\nu))}{{\rm d}(\Pi_{1}(\rho))}(x_{1})\frac{{\rm d}(\nu_{1\to 2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{1}}})}}})}{{\rm d}(\rho_{1\to 2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{1}}})}}})}(x_{2})\Big)

(8.7)

\displaystyle=H_{1}(\Pi_{1}(\nu)\mid\Pi_{1}(\rho))+\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}\Pi_{1}(\nu)({\rm d}x_{1})\,H_{2}\big(\nu_{1\to 2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{1}}})}}}\mid\rho_{1\to 2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{1}}})}}}\big)

\displaystyle\geq H_{1}(\Pi_{1}(\nu)\mid\Pi_{1}(\rho))+H_{2}\big(\Pi_{1}(\nu)\nu_{1\to 2}\mid\Pi_{1}(\nu)\rho_{1\to 2}),

where in the last step we used Jensen’s inequality for the convex function
(\nu,\rho)\mapsto H_{2}(\nu\mid\rho)
; see
[
G11
, Proposition 15.5(d)]
.
∎

Remark 8.3

(
{\rm h}\geq{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}
)

An application of (
8.6
) shows that
{\rm h}\geq{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}
, where
{\rm h}
was defined in (
2.8
), and we define
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(P)
as
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)
for any
P\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}})
, where
P
is considered as a loop-interlacement configuration with empty interlacement part. Indeed, for any
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, observe that
\Pi_{W}({\mathcal{L}})
is the direct sum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{W}=\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({\mathcal{L}})
and the set of all simple point measures that start in
W
, but have not all their particles in
W
. Using (
8.6
), we see that

H_{\Pi_{W}({\mathcal{L}})}\big(\Pi_{W}(P)\,\big|\,\Pi_{W}({{\tt Q}})\big)\geq H_{{\mathcal{L}}_{W}}\big(\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(P)\,\big|\,\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}({{\tt Q}})\big),

where we dropped on the right-hand side another entropy term.

\Diamond

9.
Finish of the proof of Theorem
2.5
: making
R\to\infty

After all the preparations, we finish the proof of Theorem
2.5
in this section: the proof of the upper bound is finished in Section
9.1
, the one of the lower bound in Section
9.2
.

9.1.
Upper bound

With the material from Sections
8
and
6.4
, we now finish the proof of the upper bound in Theorem
2.5
, based on (
6.33
). Recall that
W=W_{R}=[-R,R]^{d}
and that
\chi
was defined in (
2.24
) and the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W})
in (
6.8
). Starting from (
6.33
), it is sufficient to prove the following.

\displaystyle\liminf_{R\to\infty}\inf\Big\{\frac{1}{|W_{R}|}\langle\xi,F_{W_{R},W_{R}}\rangle+J_{W_{R}}(\xi)\colon\xi\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{W_{R}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W_{R}}),

(9.1)

\displaystyle\Pi_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(\xi)\in K_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(L),\Pi_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi)\in K_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(L),\langle\xi,\textstyle{\frac{1}{|{W_{R}}|}}{\mathfrak{N}}_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\rangle=\rho_{1},\langle\xi,\textstyle{\frac{1}{|{W_{R}}|}}{\mathfrak{N}}_{W_{R}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\rangle=\rho_{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq\inf\Big\{\langle P,F_{U}\rangle+{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)\colon P\in{\mathcal{M}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}}),\langle P,{\mathfrak{N}}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\rangle\in\overline{{\mathcal{B}}}_{2\delta}(\rho_{1}),\langle P,{\mathfrak{N}}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\rangle\in\overline{{\mathcal{B}}}_{2\delta}(\rho_{2})\Big\},

where we recall the definition of
J_{W}(\xi)
from (
6.13
). With the help of the continuity properties of
\chi
from Lemma
2.12
, the limit as
\delta\downarrow 0
of the right-hand side is easily seen to coincide with
\chi(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
, and the proof of (
2.25
) is then finished.

Recall the two sets
K_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(L)
and
K_{R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(L)
from Lemmas
6.7
and
6.8
, respectively, and note that
L
does not depend on
R
and is picked large enough such that (
6.33
) is true.

Now we turn to the proof of (
9.1
). For the ease of notation, we now write
W
instead of
W_{R}
. Assume that
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}
is an approximate minimiser in the variational formula on the left-hand side of (
9.1
), that is, it is amenable to that formula and approaches the infimum up to some quantity that vanishes as
R\to\infty
. Put

{\mathcal{H}}=\liminf_{R\to\infty}J_{W}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{\mathcal{F}}=\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{|W|}\langle\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}},F_{W,W}\rangle.

(9.2)

Without loss of generality,
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
are finite. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be given. We need to construct a
P\in{\mathcal{M}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
that is amenable for the formula on the right-hand side of (
9.1
) (i.e., satisfies
\langle P,{\mathfrak{N}}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}\rangle\in\overline{{\mathcal{B}}}_{2\delta}(\rho_{1}),
and
\langle P,{\mathfrak{N}}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}\rangle\in\overline{{\mathcal{B}}}_{2\delta}(\rho_{2})
) and such that
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)\leq{\mathcal{H}}+\varepsilon
and
\langle P,F_{U}\rangle\leq{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon
, which will finish the proof. Let
M
be so large that the constant
c_{M}
introduced in Lemma
4.3
satisfies
\log c_{M}\geq-\varepsilon/8
. Let
R
be so large that
C_{M}R^{-d/2}\leq\varepsilon/8
, where
C_{M}
is from (
5.14
). Furthermore, assume that
R
is also so large that

J_{W}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}})\leq{\mathcal{H}}+\varepsilon/8\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\frac{1}{|W|}\langle\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}},F_{W,W}\rangle\leq{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon/8.

(9.3)

In our first step, we are going to replace
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}
by loop-shred distributions in larger and larger boxes in such a way that the entropy and energy do not really increase and the expected particle densities in loops and in shreds does not really change. (In a later step, we will use Proposition
4.1
to further extend this measure to an element of
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
.) We want to use Proposition
6.6
(4) for that and Lemma
5.5
for estimating the error term, but need first to replace
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}
by an approximation that guarantees that we are allowed to apply Proposition
6.6
(4) and that the error term is small. For this, we need to employ Lemma
7.5
with fixed parameters
\mathfrak{r}\in(1,\infty)
and
S\in\mathbb{N}
and large parameters
R,M,K,L,T,\vartheta
. Put
\Theta=(\mathfrak{r},M,L,K,T,\vartheta,S)
. Pick the parameters in
\Theta
so large that the error term in Lemma
7.5
is
\leq\varepsilon/4
and pick
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W})
(namely,
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}=\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}\circ\Pi^{-1}
with the projection
\Pi
of Lemma
7.5
) that is concentrated on
A_{W;\Theta}=A_{W;\mathfrak{r},M,K,L,T}\cap({\mathcal{L}}_{W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M,\vartheta,S}})}}})
such that

J_{W;M}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}})\leq J_{W}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}})+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\frac{1}{|W|}\langle F_{W,W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}-\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}\rangle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4},

(9.4)

and
|\langle\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},\ell}})}}},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}-\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}\rangle|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}|W|

and
|\langle\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}},\ell}})}}},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}-\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}\rangle|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}|W|
and
\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}})\in K_{3R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(L+\frac{1}{4})
and
\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}})\in K_{3R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(L+\frac{1}{4})
. (The fact that all these integrals w.r.t.
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}
can be made as close to the ones w.r.t.
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}
follows from monotonicity of
\Pi
in the cutting parameters
M,K,L,\vartheta
.)

Now we apply Proposition
6.6
(4) to
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}
successively for
k=1
(producing
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,1}})}}}
, then for
k=1
and
W
replaced by
3W
(producing
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,2}})}}}
and so on. We pick the parameters
R,S
so large that the error term in Proposition
6.6
(4) (using Lemma
5.5
for the last term) is
\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}3^{-k}
for the
k
-th application of Proposition
6.6
(4). In this way, we obtain a sequence of
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{3^{k}W}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{3^{k}W})
such that the approximation error in the infimum is
\leq\varepsilon/8
; more precisely,

J_{3^{k}W;M}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+J_{W;M}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Pi_{3^{k}W\to 3^{k-1}W}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}})=\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k-1}})}}},

(9.5)

and every
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
is
3^{k}W
-shift invariant. In particular, the family
(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is consistent, and every projection of any
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
on a shift
z+W
with
z\in 2R\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is (after shifting back to the origin) equal to
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}
.

Note that
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
is concentrated on
A_{3^{k}W;\Theta}
, since its projections on any discrete shift of
W
in
3^{k}W
is equal (after shifting to the origin) to
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}
. Therefore all shreds in it have all the properties of the shreds in the configurations in
A_{W;\Theta}
(namely a bounded leg spread, a bound on the extension of all subparts, and an upper bound for the particle number in each of the boxes
W_{\mathfrak{r}}+z
). Therefore, the expected interaction
\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\langle F_{3^{k}W,3^{k}W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}\rangle
is essentially equal to
\frac{1}{|W|}\langle F_{W,W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}\rangle
, up to the interaction between any two neighbouring boxes of radius
R
, which can be upper bounded by some constant (depending only on
M,L,K,T,\mathfrak{r}
and
d,\beta,v
) times
1/R
, since it is a surface-order term. By making
R
large enough, this error term is below
\varepsilon/8
. Therefore,

\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\langle F_{3^{k}W,3^{k}W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}\rangle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{1}{|W|}\langle F_{W,W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}\rangle,\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(9.6)

By the same argument, but without error terms, the normalized expected particle numbers for loops and shreds,
\frac{1}{|W|}\langle\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}\rangle
and
\frac{1}{|W|}\langle\mathfrak{N}_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}\rangle
, do not change when going from
W
to
3^{k}W
and from
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}}
to
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
. In particular,
\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\langle\mathfrak{N}_{3^{k}W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}\rangle\in\overline{B}_{2\delta}(\rho_{1})
and
\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\langle\mathfrak{N}_{3^{k}W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{S}}}})}}},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}\rangle\in\overline{B}_{2\delta}(\rho_{2})
.

Now we use Proposition
4.1
to obtain from the consistent family
(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
some
\widetilde{P}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
that extends all the
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
s. By the
W
-shift invariance of all the
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
s,
P
is invariant under any shift by a vector
\in 2R\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Now we replace
\widetilde{P}
by
P=\frac{1}{|W|}\int_{W}{\rm d}x\,\widetilde{P}\circ\theta_{x}^{-1}
, which is shift-invariant, i.e., lies in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{L}}\times{\mathcal{S}})
. We are going to show that
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)\leq 2\varepsilon+J_{W}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}})
and
\langle F_{U},P\rangle\leq 2\varepsilon+\frac{1}{|W|}\langle F_{W,W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}\rangle
and that both the loop and the interlacement particle densities of
P
are close to
\rho_{1}
, respectively to
\rho_{2}
. This will imply (
9.1
) and hence end the proof of the upper bound in Theorem
2.5
.

It is relatively easy to see that the loop-particle density and the interlacement-particle densities lie in
{\mathcal{B}}_{3\delta}(\rho_{1})
, respectively in
{\mathcal{B}}_{3\delta}(\rho_{2})
. Indeed, use that
\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\langle\mathfrak{N}_{3^{k}W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}\rangle\sim\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\langle\mathfrak{N}_{3^{k}W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},P\rangle\to\langle\mathfrak{N}_{U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},P\rangle
as
k\to\infty
since
\langle\mathfrak{N}_{3^{k}W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},P\rangle
lies between
\langle\mathfrak{N}_{3^{k}W-W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},P\rangle
and
\langle\mathfrak{N}_{3^{k}W+W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell,{\mathcal{L}}}})}}},P\rangle
and since, according to Lemma
7.7
, the expected number of particles in box-shreds of loops is negligible with respect to the box volume. A similar argument applies to the expected particle density in interlacements; we leave the details to the reader.

Now we show that
J_{3^{k}W;M}(\Pi_{3^{k}W}(P))\leq{\mathcal{H}}+2\varepsilon
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies, via Theorem
2.3
, that
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)\leq{\mathcal{H}}+2\varepsilon
. Indeed, using convexity of
J_{3^{k}W;M}
and (
6.17
) in Proposition
6.6
(1) with
m=3
, we see that, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle J_{3^{k}W;M}(\Pi_{3^{k}W}(P))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|W|}\int_{W}{\rm d}x\,J_{3^{k}W;M}(\theta_{x}(\Pi_{x+3^{k}W}(\widetilde{P}))

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+J_{3^{k+1}W}(\Pi_{3^{k+1}W;M}(\widetilde{P}))=\varepsilon+J_{3^{k+1}W;M}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k+1}})}}})

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+J_{W;M}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,0}})}}})\leq\frac{3\varepsilon}{2}+J_{W}(\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}})\leq 2\varepsilon+{\mathcal{H}},

where we used also (
9.5
) and (
9.4
) and one of the requirements for
R
.

It is only left to show that
\langle F_{U},P\rangle\leq 2\varepsilon+{\mathcal{F}}
, where we recall the definition of
F_{U}
from (
2.22
). We first point out that, according to the spatial ergodic theorem,

\displaystyle\langle F_{U},P\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\Big\langle\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon z+U\subset 3^{k}W}F_{z+U},P\Big\rangle,

(9.7)

since
F_{U}
is bounded and has bounded reach almost surely on the support of
P
. This is seen as follows.
Recall that every
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
is concentrated on the set
A_{3^{k}W;\Theta}
of configurations; these have a globally bounded interaction between any box
z+U
and its complement
(z+U)^{\rm c}
, where ‘bounded’ is meant in terms of spatial distance, number of interacting loops/shreds and in amount of interaction. More precisely, we may pick some
C\in(0,\infty)
(only depending on the parameters in
\Theta
and on the length of the support of the interaction potential
v
), such that
F_{z+U}(\omega,\varpi)
is equal to the total interaction between
z+U
and
z+W_{C}
only, almost surely with respect to
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k}})}}}
. And if
z+U\subset 3^{k}W
, then the integration of
F_{z+U}
with respect to
P
can be replaced by the one with respect to
\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k+1}})}}}
.

Recall from (
2.21
) that
F_{W,W}(\omega,\varpi)=\frac{1}{2}F_{W,W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}(\omega)+F_{W,W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\omega,\varpi)+\frac{1}{2}F_{W,W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\varpi)
, whose definitions we recall from (
2.18
)–(
2.20
). Therefore, almost surely with respect to
P
, for any
x\in W
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we get from splitting the interaction between
3^{k}W
and
3^{k}W
into the interactions between
z+U
and
z+U^{\rm c}
and sum on
z
,

\displaystyle F_{3^{k}W,3^{k}W}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon z+U\subset 3^{k}W}\Big[\frac{1}{2}F_{z+U,z+U}+F_{z+U,W\cap(z+U^{\rm c})}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon z+U\subset W_{3^{k}-C}}\Big[\frac{1}{2}F_{z+U,z+U}+F_{z+U,z+(W_{C}\setminus U)}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon z+U\subset W_{3^{k}-C}}\Big(\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}F_{z+U,z+U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}+F_{z+U,z+U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}F_{z+U,z+U}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}

\displaystyle+F^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}})}}}_{z+U,z+(W_{C}\setminus U)}+F^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}_{z+U,z+(W_{C}\setminus U)}+F^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}_{z+(W_{C}\setminus U),z+U}+F^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}_{z+U,z+(W_{C}\setminus U)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon z+U\subset W_{3^{k}R-C}}F_{z+U},

recalling the definition of
F_{U}
from (
2.22
). Certainly, the sum of
F_{z+U}
on
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
z+U\subset 3^{k}W
but
z+U\not\subset W_{3^{k}R-C}
is negligible in (
9.7
), and

\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\Big|\Big\langle F_{3^{k}W,3^{k}W}-\frac{1}{|W|}\int_{W}{\rm d}x\,F_{x+3^{k}W,x+3^{k}W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k+1}})}}}\Big\rangle\Big|\to 0,\qquad k\to\infty.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle F_{U},P\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\big\langle F_{3^{k}W,3^{k}W},P\big\rangle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\Big\langle\int_{W}{\rm d}x\,F_{x+3^{k}W,x+3^{k}W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k+1}})}}}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{|3^{k}W|}\Big\langle F_{3^{k}W,3^{k}W},\xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W,\Theta,k+1}})}}}\Big\rangle.

Now using subsequently (
9.6
), (
9.4
) and (
9.3
), we see that
