\langle F_{U},P\rangle\leq{\mathcal{F}}+2\varepsilon
, which finishes the proof.

9.2.
Lower bound

For finishing the proof of the lower bound in Theorem
2.5
, we go back to (
6.35
). Applying Lemma
7.6
and (
2.16
), we see that, given any small
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we can make the parameters
\vartheta,M,L,K,T
and then
R
(in this order) so large that the variational formula on the right-hand side of (
6.35
) is not larger than
\varepsilon+\chi(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
, also using the continuity of
\chi(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
in
(\rho_{2},\rho_{2})
; see Lemma
2.12
. This gives, for any
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{N}|}\log\widehat{Z}_{N,R,\delta}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})}}}(\Lambda_{N},\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{C_{M,L,K,T}}{R}-\frac{C_{M}}{R^{d/2}}-\Big[\frac{C_{M,\vartheta,S}}{R}+\frac{C\vartheta^{2}}{S}\Big](\rho_{2}+\delta^{\prime})

(9.8)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon-\chi(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}).

We make now first
S\to\infty
and then
R\to\infty
, which makes the first three terms disappear. We have finished the proof of the lower bound in Theorem
2.5
.

Appendix A
The Brownian interlacement PPP with spacing
\beta

In this appendix we introduce, for
d\geq 3
, an interesting Poisson point process
{\tt R}={\tt R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({u,\beta}})}}}
of random interlacements that has some connection to the specific relative entropy
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
defined in (
2.14
), or, more directly, some connection to the kernel
{\tt K}_{W}
defined in (
2.5
). We survey its construction and some of its properties. Processes of this type were recently recognized as being instrumental in the description of the long loops in the Bose gas. In the present paper, such process does not appear in our description of the free energy, since our entropy
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
can be entirely characterised in terms of the kernel
{\tt K}_{W}
defined in (
2.5
), but it admits enlightening interpretations. Because of Lemma
A.4
below,
{\rm h}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}
can be seen as a kind of specific relative entropy with respect to the reference PPP
{\tt Q}\otimes{\tt R}
of loops and interlacements, but note the crucial appearance of the boundary-particle distribution
\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(P)
in (
2.13
). Nevertheless, the process
{\tt R}
is at the heart of the limiting description of the Bose gas and its condensate, so we decided to spend some words on it, even though it is not involved in our analysis of the interacting Bose gas.

Here is an ad-hoc definition of this process:
Let
d\geq 3
and fix
\beta\in(0,\infty)
. The
Brownian interlacement Poisson point process with particle spacing
\beta
, or for short
Brownian interlacement process
,
{\tt R}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\rm s}}})}}({\mathcal{S}})
, is the distribution of the random interlacement point process
\varpi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm B}})}}}=\sum_{g\in\Gamma_{\rm B}}\delta_{g}
, which consists of independent two-sided Brownian motions
g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
with parameter set
\mathbb{R}
, identified as equal modulo time shift by
\beta
. The only free parameter of this process is the expected total amount of particles in the unit box
U=[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{d}
, which we call
u\in(0,\infty)
. We sometimes write
{\tt R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({u,\beta}})}}}
or
{\tt R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({u}})}}}
; if we take
u=1
, then we drop this super-index.

The first construction of such a process was carried out in the seminal paper
[
Sz10
]
for simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
; later the Brownian version was constructed in
[
Sz13
]
. The version that we need in this paper is for Gaussian random walks instead, with interpolation by independent Brownian bridges. The Gaussian interlacement PPP has been already constructed in
[
AFY21
]
, but without interpolation, and without stating the distributional properties that we will need; hence we are giving a short account on the construction and on the properties that we need. Alternatively, we could also refer to the Brownian version constructed in
[
Sz13
]
and furnish each interlacement
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
independently with a determination of where
\beta\mathbb{Z}
is in the domain
\mathbb{R}
, which leads presumably to the same process that we are going to construct now, but we prefer to derive some distributional properties along our construction.

We are following the construction of the interlacement PPP for simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
given in
[
Sz10
, Section 1]
and translate it to the Gaussian random walk in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Once we have constructed this discrete-time process, we scale it up in time by a factor of
\beta
and interpolate it between the times in
\beta\mathbb{Z}
with independent Brownian bridges with time-interval
[0,\beta]
. See
[
LP18
]
for general references to the PPP theory.

Here is a slightly more explicit definition of the Gaussian interlacement PPP: It is the PPP with a
\sigma
-finite intensity measure
\Upsilon
on the set of trajectories
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
that is uniquely determined by the following property: For
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the restriction of
\Upsilon
to the set of trajectories that visit
W
is the (not normalised) measure that starts with a site
\in W
at time 0 that is ‘distributed’ according to the equilibrium measure of the Gaussian random walk on
W
, and has two independent trajectories: one into the negative time axis, conditioned on never entering
W
again, and one in the positive time axis, being just a Gaussian random walk in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. (By transience, it visits
W
only finitely often.) This
W
-dependent measure turns out to be consistent in
W
such that the described property fixes it uniquely.

Let us come to the details. By
\mathbb{P}_{x}
we denote the probability measure under which a Gaussian random walk
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
(i.e., with steps that are independent and Gaussian distributed with variance
2\beta
) starts from
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since we are in
d\geq 3
, this is a random variable with values in the set

{\mathcal{T}}_{+}=\Big\{{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\colon\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=\infty\Big\}.

By
{\mathcal{T}}=\{{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}\colon\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}x_{n}\}
we denote the set of two-sided trajectories that visit any compact set only finitely often, and by
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
its quotient space with respect to the identification of functions if they are time-shifts of each other, i.e.,
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}=\{\{\theta_{n}f\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}\colon f\in{\mathcal{T}}\}
, where
\theta_{n}
is the time-shift by
n
. By
\pi^{*}\colon{\mathcal{T}}\to{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
we denote the canonical projection. By
{\mathcal{T}}_{W}=\{(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in{\mathcal{T}}\colon x_{n}\in W\mbox{ for some }n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
we denote the set of trajectories that visit
W
, and by
{\mathcal{T}}_{W}^{*}
its image under
\pi^{*}
.

As we now need some little theory of capacities and equilibrium measures for the Gaussian random walk, we restrict to special compact subsets, namely boxes. For any box
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
{\operatorname{e}}_{W}
the density of the equilibrium measure of the Gaussian random walk, i.e.,

{\operatorname{e}}_{W}(x)={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{W}(x)\mathbb{P}_{x}(\widetilde{H}_{W}=\infty),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(A.1)

where
\widetilde{H}_{W}=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}\colon X_{n}\in W\}
is the entry time into
W
. It is clear that
{\operatorname{e}}_{W}(x)>0
in the box
W
, but
=0
outside the box.
By
\mathbb{P}_{x}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}
we denote the conditional measure
\mathbb{P}_{x}(\cdot\mid\widetilde{H}_{W}<\infty)
given that the path visits
W
. Now we introduce the path measure
Q_{W}
on
{\mathcal{T}}
as follows:

Q_{W}\big((X_{-n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in A,X_{0}\in{\rm d}x,(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in B\big)=\mathbb{P}_{x}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}(A){\operatorname{e}}_{W}(x)\mathbb{P}_{x}(B)\,{\rm d}x,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},A,B\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{+}\mbox{ mb.}

(A.2)

We also need the last time
L_{W}=\sup\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\colon X_{n}\in W\}
of a visit to
W
and the set of finite-length trajectories

{\mathcal{T}}_{W\to W}=\{{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{n})_{n\in\{0,\dots,N\}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N+1}\colon N\in\mathbb{N},x_{0}\in W,x_{N}\in W\}

(A.3)

that start and terminate in
W
.

We can now formulate and prove the existence and uniqueness of the measure
\Upsilon
that we will take as the intensity measure of our PPP.

Lemma A.1

(Existence, uniqueness and properties of
\Upsilon
)
.

(1)

There is a unique
\sigma
-finite measure
\Upsilon
on
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
such that

{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}^{*}}\,\Upsilon=\pi^{*}\circ Q_{W},\qquad W\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\mbox{ a box}.

(A.4)

(2)

\Upsilon
is invariant under time-reversal and spatial shifts.

(3)

For any
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{n})_{n\in\{0,\dots,N\}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W\to W}
,

\displaystyle Q_{W}\big((X_{-n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in A,

\displaystyle(X_{n})_{n\in\{0,\dots,L_{W}\}}\in{\rm d}{\mathbf{x}},(X_{L_{W}+n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in B\big)

(A.5)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}(A){\operatorname{e}}_{W}(x_{0})\mathbb{P}_{x_{0}}((X_{n})_{n\in\{0,\dots,N\}}\in{\rm d}{\mathbf{x}})\mathbb{P}_{x_{N}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({W}})}}}(B),\qquad A,B\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{+}\mbox{ mb.}

Proof.

We only give some few hints, as the proof is entirely analogous to the one of
[
Sz10
, Theorem 1.1]
(which is lengthy and technical). We leave the details to the reader.

The uniqueness in (1) follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
is exhausted by the sets
{\mathcal{T}}_{[-R,R]^{d}}^{*}
with
R\in\mathbb{N}
. The proof of the existence consists of showing that the measure
\Upsilon_{W}=\pi^{*}\circ Q_{W}
is consistent in
W
in the sense that, for any
W\subset\widetilde{W}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}^{*}}\,\Upsilon_{\widetilde{W}}=\Upsilon_{W}
. Then Carathéodory’s extension theorem yields (1). The proof of this is entirely analogous to the proof of the analogous assertion in
[
Sz10
, Theorem 1.1]
, with some obvious changes that come only from the continuous nature of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
: the fact that the equilibrium measure has a Lebesgue density and that trajectories run in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
rather than in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. The proof goes via a decomposition into paths that start and end in
\widetilde{W}
and run within
W
in between, and it uses the Markov property of the random walk.
∎

Assertion (1) says that
\Upsilon
can be described by the explicit formula (
A.2
) on the set of trajectories that visit
W
, and Assertion (3) says that every path under
Q_{W}
can be decomposed in time in three pieces: one piece of a Gaussian random until the first time of a visit to
W
(having the equilibrium measure as the ‘distribution’ at this time), one piece until the last visit to
W
, and the last piece, which is a Gaussian random walk conditioned on never visiting
W
anymore. These pieces are independent, given the two locations at the first and the last time of a visit to
W
.

By
{\rm Leb}
we denote the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\infty)
. Now we use the
\sigma
-finite measure
\Upsilon\otimes{\rm Leb}
on
{\mathcal{T}}^{+}\times[0,\infty)
as the intensity measure of a PPP
\varpi=\sum_{i}\delta_{(X_{i}^{*},v_{i})}
, where we are going to use the
v
-component for controlling the spatial density of the paths. More precisely, for any
v\in(0,\infty)
, we can call the point process
\sum_{i\colon v_{i}\leq v}\delta_{X_{i}^{*}}
the
Gaussian interlacement process with density parameter

v
.

Remark A.2

(Construction of the Gaussian interlacement PPP.)

From Assertion (3) in Lemma
A.1
, using the standard construction of PPPs from the intensity measure, the following construction of the PPP
\sum_{i\colon v_{i}\leq v}\delta_{X_{i}^{*}}
emerges. In simple words, one can construct the PPP on the set
{\mathcal{T}}_{W}^{*}
of paths that visit the box
W
by first sampling a Poisson-distributed variable
N
with parameter
v{\rm cap}(W)=v\int_{W}{\operatorname{e}}_{W}(x)\,{\rm d}x
and then
N
random sites
X_{0}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({1}})}}},\dots,X_{0}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({N}})}}}\in W
with distribution
{\operatorname{e}}_{W}(x)\,{\rm d}x/{\rm cap}(W)
. Then one samples independently over
i=1,\dots,N
a Gaussian random walk
(X_{-n}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
conditioned on never visiting
W
, and another, independent, Gaussian random walk
(X_{n}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
(which may return to
W
never or several times, but eventually vanishes at
\infty
). Finally, we ‘forget’ the precise time at which
X^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}
first enters
W
, i.e., we put
X_{i}^{*}=\pi^{*}(X^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}})
.

\Diamond

Now we interpolate any Gaussian random walk to a Brownian motion by inserting independent Brownian bridges and scaling them up in time with a factor of
\beta
. For this, we will be using the concept of
K
-marking with a kernel
K
, which is well-known in PPP-theory. Introduce the map

{\mathcal{T}}_{+}\ni{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mapsto B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathbf{x}}})}}}=(B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathbf{x}}})}}}_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}\in{\mathcal{C}},\qquad B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathbf{x}}})}}}_{t}=B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{n},x_{n+1}}})}}}_{t-\beta n}\quad\mbox{if }t\in[\beta n,\beta(n+1)],n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(A.6)

and
(B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({x_{n},x_{n+1}}})}}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a family of independent Brownian bridges with time-interval
[0,\beta]
from
x_{n}
to
x_{n+1}
. Then, if
X
is a Gaussian random walk with variance
2\beta
of the steps,
B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({X}})}}}
is a Brownian motion with generator
\Delta
. We can describe this interpolation operation in terms of distributions via the kernel
K
from
{\mathcal{T}}_{+}
to
{\mathcal{C}}
given by

K({\mathbf{x}},{\rm d}f)=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mu_{x_{n},x_{n+1}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\beta}})}}}\big({\rm d}(\theta_{\beta n}(f|_{[\beta n,\beta(n+1)]}))\big),\qquad{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{+},f\in{\mathcal{C}},

(A.7)

where we recall from (
1.4
) the canonical Brownian bridge measures. We use the same symbol
K
for the variants of this kernel on the sets
{\mathcal{T}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
.

From
\Upsilon
, we now proceed with the
K
-marking
\varpi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}}}=\sum_{i}\delta_{(X_{i}^{*},B_{i}^{*},v_{i})}
, where the Brownian motions
B_{i}^{*}
derive from the random walks
X_{i}^{*}
via (
A.6
), independently over
i
. Its intensity measure is
\Upsilon\otimes K\otimes{\rm Leb}
. Observe that
X_{i}^{*}
is a deterministic function of
B_{i}^{*}
and that the Brownian motions are uniquely determined only up to time-shifts in
\beta\mathbb{Z}
, in contrast to the Brownian interlacement process constructed in
[
Sz13
]
.

Finally, the measure
{\tt R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({u,\beta}})}}}
that we announced is now defined as the distribution of the process
\varpi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K,u}})}}}=\sum_{i\colon u_{i}\leq v_{u}}\delta_{B_{i}^{*}}
, where
v_{u}\in[0,\infty)
is determined by the requirement that
u=\mathbb{E}(\sum_{i\colon u_{i}\leq v_{u}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{B_{i}^{*}(n\beta)\in U})=1
, where
U=[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{d}
is the unit box. It is only technical to prove that the distribution of the process
\varpi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K,u}})}}}
is invariant under time-reversal and spatial shifts, since the Brownian bridge measures have the relevant properties for that. That is, the distribution
{\tt R}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({u,\beta}})}}}
of
\varpi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K,u}})}}}
lies in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm s}})}}}({\mathcal{S}})
. This finishes the introduction of the Brownian
\beta
-spaced interlacement process.

Remark A.3

(Construction of the Brownian
\beta
-spaced interlacement PPP.)

Referring to the construction of Remark
A.2
, the
B_{i}^{*}
may be constructed from the
X^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}
by interpolating them by Brownian bridges as in (
A.6
), and
N
independent standard Brownian motions
B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}=(B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
with generator
\Delta
arise, starting from
B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{0}=X_{0}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}
. Analogously, and using time-reversal properties of the Brownian bridge measures, we interpolate also the negative-time parts and obtain Brownian motions conditioned on never hitting
W
at any time
\in\beta\mathbb{N}
, independent of the positive-time parts, given the starting sites
B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{0}
. Finally, we ‘forget’ the integer-part of
\frac{1}{\beta}
times the time, i.e., we put
B_{i}^{*}=\pi^{*}(B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}})=\{\theta_{\beta n}(B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}})\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

\Diamond

Now we finally give the connection between the interlacement process
{\tt R}
and our analysis of the interacting Bose gas. Recall the kernel
{\tt K}_{W}
from (
2.5
) and recall from (
2.13
) how crucial it is in this connection.

Lemma A.4

(Identification of
{\tt K}_{W}
in terms of
{\tt R}
)
.

The kernel
{\tt K}_{W}(\mu,\cdot)
defined in (
2.5
) is equal to the kernel
\Pi_{W}({{\tt R}})_{{\mathcal{T}}_{W}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{W}}(\mu,\cdot)
.

Proof.

We refer to the construction of the Brownian
\beta
-spaced interlacement process
\varpi
from Remarks
A.2
and
A.3
. The event
\{\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\varpi)=\mu\}
on that we condition depends only on the processes
(B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
, i.e., the parts from the time of the first visit to
W
at a time
\in\beta\mathbb{Z}
on. Introduce the subsequent entry and exit times (up to the factor
\beta
)
0=\tau^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{1}<\sigma_{1}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}<\tau_{2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}<\sigma_{2}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}<\dots<\tau_{m_{i}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}<\sigma_{m_{i}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}<\infty
of
B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}
, and put
\mu=\sum_{j\in I}\delta_{(x_{j},l_{j},y_{j})}
, then the event
\{\partial\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\varpi)=\mu\}
is equal to the disjoint union over all bijections
h\colon J=\{(i,k)\in[N]\times\mathbb{N}\colon k\leq m_{i}\}\to I
of the event

\bigcap_{(i,k)\in J}\Big\{B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{\tau^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{k}\beta}=x_{h(i,k)},\sigma_{k}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}-\tau_{k}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}=l_{h(i,k)},B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{\sigma^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{k}\beta}=y_{h(i,k)}\Big\}.

On this event,
\Pi_{W}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\mathcal{S}}}})}}}(\varpi)
is the empirical measure of the paths
(B^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{t})_{t\in[\beta\tau^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{k},\beta\sigma^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}}_{k}]}
, properly time-shifted, with
(i,k)\in J
. According to the strong Markov property at all these stopping times, these path pieces are conditionally independent, and their distribution is given by
q_{x_{h(i,k)},y_{h(i,k)}}^{{{\scriptscriptstyle{({l_{h(i,k)},W}})}}}
. Hence, their joint distribution is the product measure of these, which is
{\tt K}_{W}(\mu,\cdot)
. This finishes the proof.
∎
